Assert a **new** `listing_score` row with a changed `inputs_digest`, a changed `decision_digest` (the payload embeds `inputs_json`), and an identical `deal_score`. A negative control rewrites the same field to a byte-identical value and asserts zero writes.

**B5 test (SA-003, MS-2d, live DB).** Re-resolve a member listing to a **different `product_variant` at the same grain**, leaving its cohort key unchanged. Assert `member_projection_digest` changes, B5 fires, and — because the resolution id is in group 3 of `D` — that listing's `inputs_digest` changes and it is rescored, while cohort peers whose own `D` is unchanged are not.

#### 3.4.2 Write-on-change, keyed on the whole decision

Review finding SA-006 of audit `9f6079cb`, blocking (not audit `b2eedc33`’s SA-006, which is the serialization contract at the end of this section): appending a row only when `deal_score`, the cap set, `is_provisional`, or `algorithm_version` changed leaves the current row describing obsolete inputs whenever a subscore, `$/TB`, cohort key, matcher grain, risk flag, seller evidence, margin, or explanation text moves without shifting the rounded score. DR-004 requires the stored row to be the most recent automated decision, not merely a matching number.

**Rule.** `ListingScore.decision_digest` is `SHA-256` over the canonical JSON of the **complete DR-004 decision payload**: `inputs_json` (which itself contains everything the §3.4.1 fingerprint covers, plus provenance records), all four subscores, `base_score`, `cap_applied` and `cap_reasons`, `deal_score`, `dollars_per_tb`, `price_basis`, `quantity` and `quantity_basis`, `n_eff`, `lambda_shrinkage`, `is_provisional`, `provisional_reasons`, `cohort_key`, the `cohort_baseline` **version** id (§3.3.5 — it moves exactly when `baseline_digest` moves, so it adds no write amplification), `relaxation_step`, `relaxation_exhausted`, `matcher_grain`, `risk_flags`, thresholds and margins, `explanation_json`, `explanation_text`, and `algorithm_version`.

A new row is appended **iff** the freshly computed `decision_digest` differs from the current row's. Zero writes occur **only** when the complete canonical decision is byte-identical.

**Two listing-row writes, on two schedules — the same split §3.3.5(b) makes for a cohort** (revision 8, review finding SA-001). The S2-1 pointer columns `current_deal_score`, `current_score_provisional`, `current_scored_at` and `current_cohort_key` are a copy of the appended row, so they move **only when a row is appended**, in that same transaction. `current_offer_observed_at` (§2.1) is a **detection stamp**, not a copy, so it is rewritten on **every** successful evaluation of the listing, appended row or not — otherwise an identical re-poll would leave C9 permanently true (§2.2.1). The two may therefore legitimately disagree, exactly as `cohort_baseline_current.last_evaluated_at` may run ahead of `current_version.computed_at`, and a reader must not treat the disagreement as drift.

`ListingScore.offer_observed_at` is the score row's own provenance record of the observation it was taken against. It is **not** in `inputs_json`, **not** in `inputs_digest` and **not** in `decision_digest` — a decision does not change because the same facts were re-observed — so it never contributes to the write-on-change test. This is the one field on the row that the decision digest deliberately does not cover, and the reason is stated in §3.4.1 group 1.

**Population invariants MS-2d asserts after a refresh**, so a drift between the candidate comparand, the evidence and the row is caught by a whole-population check rather than by a single fixture: for every listing in `P`, `current_offer_observed_at` equals the `observed_at` the live §2.2.1 selector returns (or is NULL exactly when the selector returns no eligible row); and for every current `listing_score` row, `offer_observed_at` is `≤ current_offer_observed_at`, with equality on every listing whose facts have not been re-observed since its last write.

##### The write transaction's serialization contract (review finding SA-006 of audit `b2eedc33`, blocking)

Revision 8 introduced a **mutable** `current_offer_observed_at` stamp and made a re-polled listing a candidate, and then specified interleavings only for `ListingScore` deletion versus refresh (§2.3b). It said nothing about `OfferSnapshot` deletion versus refresh, and the two jobs are independently scheduled: `max_instances=1` is a **per job id** guarantee (`src/hw_radar/acquisition/scheduling/apply.py:14-19` states this in as many words), and `poller/service.py:337-342` registers `retention-sweep` as its own job beside the others, so `score-refresh` and `retention-sweep` can be inside their write paths at the same time. `purge_expired` deletes each model in independently selected batches, one `transaction.atomic()` per batch, and coordinates with no future scoring transaction (`catalog/management/commands/purge_expired.py:213-223`). The race that leaves is real and permanent: the refresh reads `S`, the sweep deletes `S` and clears the listing, and the refresh then wins the `listing` update and restores `current_offer_observed_at = S.observed_at` — or appends a score computed from `S` and points `current_scored_at` at it. **C9 cannot repair it**, because no surviving snapshot has an `observed_at` greater than the restored stamp; **C1 cannot repair it**, because `current_scored_at` was restored too. The listing keeps a current decision derived from deleted bounded-retention evidence indefinitely, which is a correctness failure and, for an eBay listing, a §2.3 boundary failure.

**The invariant, normative for both jobs and for every future writer:**

> **Every selected input of `D` has an *anchor row* that both its deleter and its consumer must lock**, and the anchor is chosen so that it exists whether or not the affected set does. For a `D` term selected from rows keyed by a **fixed parent** the anchor is that parent: `offer_snapshot` anchors on its `listing`. For a term selected from rows whose affected set is a **mutable predicate** the anchor is the row the predicate is defined *by*: `seller_rating_observation` anchors on its **`seller`** row, never on the current membership of `P`. A transaction may **delete** a row any listing's `D` selects only while holding `FOR UPDATE` on that row's anchor; a refresh may **select** such a term only while holding at least `FOR SHARE` on the same anchor; and a refresh may **write** a `listing_score` row or any `listing.current_*` column only while holding `FOR UPDATE` on that listing. **One lock order everywhere, over lock *classes* and not merely over rows: `seller` → `listing` → any other row, ascending by `id` within each class.**

**The invariant was too narrow in revision 9, and revision 10 widens it (review finding SA-007 of audit `b2eedc33`, blocking).** Revision 9 wrote the delete clause as "an `offer_snapshot` row … its parent `listing` row", which named the FK parent of one dependency rather than the property that makes the lock necessary. `SellerRatingObservation` has no FK to `listing` at all — its fan-out is "every listing in `P` for that seller" — and revision 9's own sweep hook already locked exactly that set, so the *lock* half was right while the *rule* did not say why. Revision 10 generalized it to **the set of listings whose decision the deleted evidence feeds** — and revision 11 has to go one step further, because **that is still a set, and a row lock cannot lock a row that does not exist yet** (review finding SA-009 of audit `b2eedc33`, blocking).

**Why locking the matching listings is not enough, in the reviewer's own interleaving.** `listings in P for seller S` is a *mutable predicate*, not a fixed FK edge. A listing with no snapshot is out of `P`; the sweep selects and locks the current fan-out; `append_snapshot` then gives that listing its first snapshot and puts it into `P` **without touching `Listing` and without any lock** (`acquisition/persist.py:74-102`, and `acquisition/pipeline.py:240-252` performs the upsert, the relist and the append as separate calls with no transaction around the population-entry sequence); a refresh now locks that newly eligible listing — a row the sweep never selected and therefore never locked — re-derives the *same* seller aggregate because the sweep's `DELETE` is not committed and `R` is still visible under READ COMMITTED, passes `D_selected`, and writes a `listing_score` whose `inputs_json` carries `R`. The sweep then commits. L9's clear repairs the *pointer* and cannot touch the appended row, which survives for its own S2-2a TTL — precisely the residual §3.4.2 rejected the abandon-only protocol for. **Seller reassignment produces the same phantom** (`Listing.seller` is a mutable, nullable FK, `catalog/models/market.py:241-243`), and `D_selected` cannot close either one: the phantom's re-derivation is *correct* against the database it can see, so the comparison passes. **This is the case that proves revalidation and locking are different tools** — no amount of re-reading fixes a writer that was never serialized against the deleter.

**The fix is to stop anchoring on the set.** The affected listings change; the **`seller` row does not**, and every path that can add a listing to the fan-out — first-snapshot `P` entry, seller reassignment, relist — reaches the fan-out only by naming that seller. `Seller` is an existing, stable, per-marketplace identity row (`catalog/models/market.py:64-77`), so it is available as an anchor with no schema change. Anchoring there makes membership irrelevant to correctness: a phantom cannot consume `R` because it cannot *select* the aggregate without the anchor lock the sweep is holding.

That order is the one this repository already uses and already documents: `matching/resolver.py:447-449` locks the listing row and then the current resolution edge ("CR-002: serialize concurrent resolutions of one listing"), and `acquisition/scheduling/apply.py:165-168` states the same discipline for its own two rows ("Lock order is config row then lane row, in this order on every path"). MS-2 adopts it rather than inventing a scoring-specific scheme.

**(1) The refresh.** Reads and computation stay **outside** any lock — they are the expensive part and they are read-only. The write is then one transaction **per listing** (§3.4.2 already puts the append and the pointer update in one transaction, and its unit is one listing):

```text
BEGIN
  SELECT id FROM seller  WHERE id = :seller_id  FOR SHARE      -- (a0) anchor, when seller_id IS NOT NULL
  SELECT id FROM listing WHERE id = :listing_id FOR UPDATE     -- (a)  anchor + serialization point
  re-derive D_selected for :listing_id                         -- (b)  inside both locks, after them
  if D_selected ≠ the D_selected the decision was built from:  -- (c)  one comparison, one branch
      ROLLBACK                                                 --      abandon; write nothing
  INSERT the listing_score row (if §3.4.2 says a row is due)
  UPDATE listing SET current_* / current_offer_observed_at     -- (d)  only on the committing branch
COMMIT
```

- **(a0) is revision 11 and it is what closes SA-009.** The refresh takes the **seller anchor first**, before the listing, because the global order is `seller` → `listing`. `FOR SHARE` and not `FOR UPDATE`: what the refresh needs is that the seller's rating set cannot change under it, which is a read lock — and a big eBay seller has many listings, each scored in its own transaction, so an exclusive anchor would serialize the whole seller through one row while a shared one lets them all proceed. The sweep's `FOR UPDATE` on the same row conflicts with every one of them, which is exactly the required behaviour. A listing with `seller_id IS NULL` (`catalog/models/market.py:241-243`) takes no anchor and skips (a0); it has no seller aggregate to protect, and it acquires one only by a `Listing` write, which is C3.
- **The seller is re-read from the locked listing row and is part of `D_selected`.** (a0) locks the seller the *unlocked* computation saw, and a reassignment could have moved it in between — so `listing.seller_id` re-read at (b) from the row (a) locks is compared like every other term, and a mismatch abandons at (c). The listing is then reselected by **C3**, because `seller` is in `SCORING_INPUT_FIELDS` (§2.1). Without this the refresh could hold the *wrong* seller's anchor and still commit, which would reintroduce SA-009 through the reassignment door rather than the `P`-entry one.
- **What (a0) guarantees, stated exactly rather than aspirationally.** Every refresh of a listing of seller `S` either **completes entirely before** the sweep acquires `S`'s anchor, or **begins entirely after** the sweep commits. No refresh straddles the deletion. The first kind writes a score from evidence that was live and uncommitted-to-deletion at the time — the ordinary lifecycle, since every score is eventually outlived by its evidence, which is what S2-2a governs — and L9's clear then runs *after* it and repairs its pointer. The second kind sees the survivors. **The claim this design makes is therefore "no score is written from evidence whose deletion has already been decided, and no L9 clear can be overwritten", not "no score is ever written from evidence that will later be deleted"** — the second is unachievable and stating it would be the kind of false invariant this section exists to avoid.

- **`D_selected` is the whole revalidation contract, and it is *one* step, not one per dependency (review finding SA-007).** Revision 9 wrote step (b) as "re-run the §2.2.1 selector", which covered group 1 and silently left group 3's seller evidence — chosen by §3.2.5(d)'s own selector over rows the sweep deletes on the same six-hour clock — validated by nothing. The contract is now stated as a property of `D` rather than as a list of selectors:

  > **`D_selected` is the sub-document of `D` produced by a selector over rows the refresh does not lock while computing.** Every such term is re-derived inside the write transaction, after the lock, and compared **as canonical JSON under §3.4.2's canonicalization** against the value the decision was built from. A difference in **any** term abandons the **whole** write. There is one comparison, one branch, and one definition of "the evidence moved".

  Comparing the canonical sub-document rather than a per-selector identity is what makes it one step and what makes it total. It compares **selected identity and normalized value together**, which matters because §3.2.5(d)'s fallbacks are not all identities: a seller whose last surviving observation is deleted falls through to the **S2-12 source prior**, where there is no row to name — but there *is* a group-3 seller sub-document, and it differs. An identity-only check would read "absent, absent" and pass.

- **Its members today, enumerated so the rule is checkable rather than aspirational** — and the enumeration is a consequence of the two conditions above, not a second list to maintain:

  | `D` term | Selector | In `D_selected`? |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Group 1 offer facts | §2.2.1 `S(listing)` over `offer_snapshot` | **Yes** — the revision-9 case |
  | Group 3 seller aggregate (`p_obs`, `n`, `y`, `count_basis`, `is_usable`, the selected `observed_at`) | §3.2.5(d) over `seller_rating_observation`, falling through to the S2-12 prior | **Yes** — the revision-10 case (SA-007) |
  | Group 2 extraction provenance, and the group-3 facts stored **on the `listing` row** (`returns_policy`, `returns_window_days`, `seller`, `source_site`) | None — read directly from the locked row | **Re-read, not re-selected.** They live on the row (a) locks, so the refresh re-reads them from the locked tuple in the same statement and includes them in the comparison for free. This also re-checks §2.2's `P` membership (`delisted_at`, `expires_at`) under the lock, so a refresh cannot restore a `current_*` pointer for a listing the sweep's `redact_expired` just took out of `P` in the transaction it was waiting on |
  | Group 3 catalog facts (`drive_spec`, `product_model`, `product_variant`) and `source_config.seller_policy_prior` | Reached by join, not selected from a deletable set | **No, and the reason is a class filter rather than an absence.** `ProductModel` and `DriveSpec` *are* `RetentionGoverned` (`catalog/models/identity.py:131,206`), so they are in the sweeper's registry — but `purge_expired` only ever selects rows whose `retention_class` is in `BOUNDED_RETENTION_CLASSES` (`purge_expired.py:_expired`), and §3.4.1's coverage table makes MS-2a assert these are `merchant_fact`. Registry membership is not deletability. An **operator** deletion is outside every automated path and is already named in §6 with the `score_refresh --force-cohort` remedy |
  | Group 4 baseline evidence snapshot | The cohort pointer's `current_version` | **No — the database holds it instead.** The version is `merchant_fact` and the §3.3.5(f) prune deletes only **uncited** versions; the refresh's own `INSERT` of a citing `listing_score` row takes the FK's `KEY SHARE` lock on that version, so a concurrent prune `DELETE` blocks until this transaction commits and then fails on `PROTECT` — the `ProtectedError` path §3.3.5(f) already carries. A *promotion* between computation and commit is not a deletion race and needs no term here: the written score cites the old version id, the pointer names the new one, and **L1/C2 select the listing on the next tick** because L1 compares with `IS DISTINCT FROM` |
  | `listing_resolution` (current edge) | Selected by `is_current` | **No.** `ListingResolution` is a plain `models.Model` with `on_delete=CASCADE` from `Listing` (`catalog/models/resolution.py:35-36`) — it is not `RetentionGoverned`, so no sweep deletes it, and it dies only with the listing that has already left `P` |

- **The rule a later author applies to a new dependency**, stated as the sibling of the candidate-clause rule §3.4.1 already carries. That one says *a `D` member that lives on a row other than `Listing` needs its own candidate clause, because `scoring_inputs_changed_at` can only describe `Listing` writes.* Its commit-time twin: **a `D` member chosen by a selector over rows a sweep may delete needs its own term in `D_selected`, because a candidate clause only detects a change on a later tick and cannot make this transaction's write correct.** SA-007 is exactly what happens when a dependency gets the first and not the second — L9 fired, and the refresh overwrote it.
- **Both halves are load-bearing and neither substitutes for the other.** The lock stops a delete *after* the re-derivation; the re-derivation catches a sweep that committed *before* the lock was granted, because the computation that built the decision ran unlocked. Dropping either one reopens SA-006 and SA-007 together.
- **An abandoned write loses nothing, and that is provable rather than hopeful.** No `current_*` column — `current_offer_observed_at` included — is written outside (d), so an abandoned evaluation cannot advance any comparand past evidence it did not use. The listing is still selected on the next tick, and the case analysis is now per member of `D_selected`. **Group 1:** **C1** if the L8 hook cleared it, **C9** on the `COALESCE` branch if it is on the `price_unavailable` path, **C9** by ordinary comparison if a newer eligible row arrived. **Group 3 seller:** the aggregate can only have moved because a rating was deleted or a newer one was written — the first fired **L9**, whose clear leaves `current_scored_at` NULL and is therefore **C1**, and the second is **C5**. That C5 detects only a *newer* observation is exactly why the backwards direction must be C1 and not C5, and why L9's clear must survive: this is the guarantee revision 9 left resting on a race.
- **An insert can still land between (b) and the commit** — `append_snapshot` takes no lock, deliberately (§2.2.1's rejected writer-side stamp), and neither does MS-2e's rating writer. The written score then cites slightly older evidence while a newer row exists, and **C9** (offer) or **C5** (seller) fires on the next tick. That is the ordinary self-healing path, not a race: an insert only ever adds a *newer* row, which is what those two clauses are for. Deletion is the direction that cannot self-heal, which is why it — and only it — needs the lock.

**(2) The sweep, and the one hook it gains.** `purge_expired`'s batch loop already knows the batch's primary keys before it deletes them (`purge_expired.py:213-221`), so the parent lock is a fourth optional classmethod under the **same** discovery protocol the sweeper already uses for `deletion_exempt_q` and `redact_expired` (`getattr(model, ...)`, `purge_expired.py:127,144`) — the sweeper still gains no per-model special case:

- **`Model.lock_parents_for_delete(pks)`** — called inside the batch's existing `transaction.atomic()`, **before** the `DELETE`. `OfferSnapshot` implements it as `SELECT id FROM listing WHERE id IN (distinct listing_ids of the batch) ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE`. `ListingScore` implements it identically. **`SellerRatingObservation` takes two classes, in the global order** (revision 11, review finding SA-009): first `SELECT id FROM seller WHERE id IN (distinct seller_ids of the batch) ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE` — the anchor, which is what actually makes the deletion safe — then the listing rows of the fan-out, `ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE`.

  - **The listing half is a fan-out, not a parent lookup, and it costs what revision 10 said it costs.** Deleting `k` rating rows can lock `m ≫ k` listing rows, because one eBay seller may hold many listings in `P`. It is not new work: that `m` is precisely the set L9's clear was already going to `UPDATE` inside the same transaction (§3.4.1), so the lock set equals the write set and the hook only takes the locks earlier and in a defined order.
  - **But the listing half is no longer load-bearing for correctness, and saying so is the substance of the SA-009 fix.** The anchor is what stops a phantom from consuming the deleted rating; the listing locks exist so that L9's *cross-row* guard is evaluated outside the READ COMMITTED re-check described below. It follows that the membership query **does not have to be complete** — which is fortunate, because it cannot be. A listing that enters `P` after the query cannot have a current score citing the deleted rating (it had none to cite), and it cannot acquire one, because its refresh must first take the anchor. **The clear set is therefore defined by a stored fact — which scores cite a deleted rating — and only *bounded* by the membership query**, which is an index lookup rather than the correctness argument.
  - **The two listings the membership query legitimately misses, enumerated so the completeness claim is checkable.** A listing scored from the deleted rating that has since **left `P`** is not in the query — §2.3(b) clears its `current_*` unconditionally on delist or redaction, so it cannot keep a stale pointer. A listing **reassigned away** from the swept seller between the score and the sweep is not in the query either — that reassignment is a `Listing` write over `SCORING_INPUT_FIELDS`, so **C3** selects it on the next tick. Neither needs the sweep to have found it.
- **Why `ListingScore` and `SellerRatingObservation` take it too, when §2.3(b) already argued their clears are safe unlocked.** So that there is **one** order rather than two arguments — and because the pre-lock buys a second property their guards need. §2.3(b)'s `AND current_scored_at = %(scored_at)s` compares a column of *the very row being updated*; L9's guard (§3.4.1) compares the deleted row against **the score's stored group-3 `observed_at`**, which lives on a different row. Under PostgreSQL's documented READ COMMITTED behaviour an `UPDATE` that blocks on a concurrently updated row re-checks its qualification against the new row version, but sub-selects inside that qualification are still evaluated against the **command's original snapshot** — the documented consequence that an updating command "can see the effects of concurrent updating commands on the same rows it is trying to update, but it does not see effects of those commands on other rows in the database". Taking the **anchor** lock first — the `listing` for `ListingScore`, the `seller` *and then* the listing fan-out for `SellerRatingObservation` (revision 12 makes that wording exact, SA-009) — means the hook's predicate is evaluated in a fresh statement after the locks are granted, so it never goes through that re-check at all. §2.3(b)'s guard is unchanged and remains independently sufficient for its own case; the pre-lock is what makes the *cross-row* guards sound.
- **The same rule is why the refresh cannot replace (a)+(b) with a clever `WHERE EXISTS(the snapshot still exists)` guard on its own `UPDATE`**, and it is stated here so a later author does not "simplify" the lock away. `offer_snapshot` is another row, so the re-check can return true against a snapshot the sweep has already deleted. This is the precise reason SA-006 needs a lock while §2.3(b)'s SA-002 did not.

**(3) Deadlock, and why there is no cycle — the argument changes in revision 11 and the old one is withdrawn, not patched.** Revisions 9–10 argued from a **one-directional wait graph**: the refresh held exactly one `listing` lock and afterwards took no lock the sweep held, so the sweep could wait for the refresh and never the reverse. **That argument dies with the seller anchor**, because the refresh now takes two locks and the sweep takes the same two. Left as it was, the order would have been inverted — refresh holding `listing` and wanting `seller`, sweep holding `seller` and wanting `listing` — which is a textbook cycle, and it is the reason (a0) is placed *before* (a) rather than after it.

The replacement is the standard and stronger argument: **a single total order over lock classes, with ascending `id` within each class, observed by every writer without exception.**

| Writer | Classes it takes, in order |
| --- | --- |
| Score refresh (§3.4.2) | `seller` `FOR SHARE` (when `seller_id IS NOT NULL`) → its one `listing` `FOR UPDATE` |
| `SellerRatingObservation` sweep | `seller` `FOR UPDATE` (ascending) → fan-out `listing` `FOR UPDATE` (ascending) |
| `OfferSnapshot` sweep, `ListingScore` sweep | `listing` `FOR UPDATE` (ascending) — one class only |
| `Listing.mark_delisted()` / `redact_expired()` (§2.3b) | `listing` — one class only |

No writer takes `listing` before `seller`, and none takes two rows of one class out of ascending order, so the wait-for graph has no cycle. The order **extends** the two orders this repository already documents rather than contradicting either: `matching/resolver.py:447-449` takes `listing` → resolution edge, which is a suffix of it, and `acquisition/scheduling/apply.py:165-168` takes `SourceConfig` → `SourceLaneState`, a disjoint pair no path here touches. Two sweeps still cannot cycle against each other: `retention-sweep` is a single APScheduler job id under `max_instances=1` (`poller/service.py:337-342`). The §3.3.5(g) baseline prune remains outside the argument by construction — it takes neither class.

**The one cost the anchor adds, named rather than buried.** `FOR SHARE` on a `seller` row blocks a concurrent `UPDATE` of that row, so an MS-1 write to `Seller.name` / `normalized_name` / `notes` can wait behind a refresh transaction. `Seller` rows are near-static — created on first sight and rarely rewritten — and the wait is bounded by one listing's write transaction, the same bound the sweep already accepts. If MS-2d measures it as material, the named alternative is an advisory lock keyed on the seller id, which has the same semantics without touching the row; it is not adopted now because a real row lock is visible in `pg_locks` and needs no key-collision argument.

**(4) Isolation level, stated rather than assumed.** The protocol is specified against PostgreSQL's default **READ COMMITTED**, which is what this project runs: `src/hw_radar/settings.py:88-95` configures `django.db.backends.postgresql` with no `OPTIONS` isolation override, and Django does not change the server default. Correctness rests on the explicit `FOR UPDATE` locks and on step (c)'s re-comparison, **not** on snapshot semantics, so raising the level would not be required — and if a future deployment did raise it to `REPEATABLE READ` or `SERIALIZABLE`, the refresh would additionally have to treat a serialization failure as the (c) branch: abandon the write and let the next tick re-select. That is one `except` clause, and it is recorded here so the change is a known amendment rather than a surprise.

**(5) The L8 hook's guard, stated because this protocol depends on it.** `OfferSnapshot.invalidate_listing_scores(deleted_keys)` (§3.4.1) issues, per deleted `(listing_id, observed_at)` pair, the same shape as §2.3(b)'s clear and L9's:

```sql
UPDATE listing
   SET current_deal_score = NULL, current_score_provisional = NULL,
       current_scored_at = NULL, current_cohort_key = NULL,
       current_offer_observed_at = NULL
 WHERE id = %(listing_id)s
   AND current_offer_observed_at = %(observed_at)s
```

A deleted **non-selected** row therefore updates nothing and costs no fan-out, exactly as L9's non-selected case does. A `price_unavailable` listing has `current_offer_observed_at IS NULL`, so no deleted snapshot ever clears it — and that is correct, not a gap, because such a row consumed no snapshot fact at all (§3.4.1 group 1, review finding SA-005). The two revision-9 fixes meet here, and the L8 test asserts the meeting point rather than each half separately.

**(6) L9's guard is the same shape, and revision 10 says what it now rests on (review finding SA-007).** `SellerRatingObservation.invalidate_seller_scores(deleted_keys)` clears the same five columns for every listing in `P` of an affected seller, guarded on the score's stored group-3 `observed_at` equalling a deleted row's (§3.4.1). Two properties that were implicit are now stated, because SA-007 turned on both:

- **The guard is cross-row** — it reads `listing_score` while updating `listing` — so, unlike §2.3(b)'s same-row guard, it is sound only because `lock_parents_for_delete` takes the listing locks first and the predicate is then evaluated in a fresh statement rather than through the READ COMMITTED re-check described in (2).
- **The clear is one-time and has no C-clause backstop in its own direction.** After it fires, the listing is repaired by **C1** (`current_scored_at IS NULL`) and by nothing else: **C5 looks only for an observation *newer* than the one the score recorded**, and a deletion moves the aggregate *backwards* — to an older surviving row, to an unusable row, or to the S2-12 prior. So a refresh that restores `current_scored_at` after L9 fired does not merely lose a tick; it removes the only repair path, which is exactly the indefinite staleness SA-007 names. `D_selected`'s group-3 term is what prevents that restore, and the two directions — L9 clears, revalidation refuses to un-clear — are the same guarantee written from both ends.

*Rejected alternative — no locks; let the refresh abandon on a stale re-read only.* It is one fewer moving part and it does close the indefinite-staleness failure: the sweep's L8 clear would simply run after the refresh committed, leaving no current score and rescoring on the next tick. It is rejected because of the row it leaves behind: the refresh would mint a **fresh** `listing_score` row whose `inputs_json` carries a just-deleted eBay price, and that row then lives for its own S2-2a TTL — extending the life of evidence the sweep was deleting, which is the one thing §2.3 exists to prevent. **What would reopen it:** if MS-2d measures the sweep's wait on refresh locks as material against the retention sweep's own budget, the abandon-only protocol is the named fallback, and its residual — one TTL of a just-deleted price — is the cost that has to be accepted explicitly.

*Rejected alternative — a monotonic invalidation counter on `listing` that L8 increments and the refresh must echo.* SA-006 offers it and it is sound, but it buys nothing this protocol does not already have while costing a column, a migration, and a second definition of "the current offer moved" living beside `current_offer_observed_at` — the duplication §2.2.1 rejected the writer-side stamp for. The selector's own output is already the exact comparand, and it needs no writer to maintain it.

**Tests (MS-2d, live DB, two connections with explicit barriers — SA-006's and SA-007's `suggested_validation` in full).** Cases 1–6 are the offer half (SA-006), cases 7–11 the seller-deletion half (SA-007), and cases 12–15 the fan-out **phantom** half (SA-009). Each asserts the same three post-conditions: every `current_*` pointer names **surviving** evidence or is NULL; the current `listing_score` row was computed from surviving evidence; and the **next quiet tick** selects the listing at most once and then performs **no semantic write** — no new `listing_score` row once the repair tick has run.

1. **Refresh reads `S`, then the sweep deletes it.** Barrier after the refresh's computation and before its write transaction. Assert the refresh **abandons** at step (c) — zero `listing_score` rows written — that the sweep's L8 clear leaves all five `current_*` columns NULL, and that the next tick's **C1** rescores the listing exactly once against the newest survivor.
2. **The sweep selects its batch, then the refresh commits first.** Barrier after `_batches` yields the pks and before `lock_parents_for_delete`. Assert the refresh's write commits, that the sweep then blocks on the `listing` lock rather than proceeding, and that after it unblocks the delete and the L8 clear both land — leaving no current score — and the next tick rescores against survivors. Assert the sweep's `DELETE` never ran while the refresh held the lock.
3. **The fact-identical repoll (SA-006's second half).** With `ListingScore.offer_observed_at` deliberately trailing `listing.current_offer_observed_at` (§3.4.2's stated legitimate disagreement), delete the snapshot the **stamp** names and assert the L8 guard fires on the stamp — not on the score row's trailing value — and that the whole-population invariant at the end of this section still holds afterwards.
4. **Negative control — a deleted non-selected snapshot.** Assert zero `listing` rows updated, zero rescores, and an unchanged `current_scored_at`; the deletion still reaches the cohort through B3.
5. **Negative control — a `price_unavailable` listing losing a NULL-`fx_rate` snapshot.** Assert the L8 guard matches nothing, the current score is untouched, and its `decision_digest` is unchanged, because no snapshot fact was in its `D` (SA-005).
6. **Deadlock control.** Run the sweep over a batch spanning many listings against a refresh looping over the same listings for 30 s and assert **zero** `deadlock detected` errors and zero failed `ScoringRun` rows; assert the sweep completed and every affected listing ends with a current score derived from surviving evidence.

The seller half repeats the same two orderings against `seller_rating_observation`, and each of its fallback shapes is a separate fixture because §3.2.5(d)'s post-delete aggregate differs in kind across them. Every case seeds **five** listings in `P` for one eBay seller, so the fan-out is asserted rather than assumed, and every case additionally asserts that the score's group-3 seller sub-document, the seller subscore, the S2-12 cap outcome and the explanation's seller clause all name the surviving evidence:

7. **Refresh computes from rating `R`, then the sweep deletes `R` — fallback to an older *usable* row.** Barrier after the refresh's computation and before its write transaction. Assert the refresh **abandons** at step (c) on the **group-3 term** with the group-1 offer selection **unchanged** — which is the assertion that fails against revision 9 and is therefore the regression guard for SA-007 — zero `listing_score` rows written, L9's clear standing on all five listings, and the next tick's **C1** rescoring each of the five exactly once against the older surviving row.
8. **The sweep selects and deletes `R` before the refresh commits — fallback to an *unusable* row.** Barrier after `_batches` yields the pks and before `lock_parents_for_delete`. Assert the sweep blocks on the `listing` locks rather than proceeding, that after it unblocks the delete and L9's clear both land, and that the repair tick produces the §3.2.5(d) unusable-row aggregate with its `seller_*` risk flag — **not** the deleted row's `p_obs`, and **not** the prior.
9. **No surviving observation — fallback to the S2-12 source prior.** Delete the seller's only rating. Assert the repair tick's group-3 seller sub-document is the prior with `seller_no_rating`, and assert explicitly that step (c) caught this case: it is the one where **selected identity is absent on both sides**, so an identity-only comparison would have passed and the canonical-sub-document comparison is what refuses it. This fixture is the reason the comparand is the sub-document rather than the row id.
10. **Negative control — a deleted *non-selected* older rating.** Assert L9's guard matches nothing, zero `listing` rows are updated, the refresh's step (c) **passes** (the aggregate is unchanged, because §3.2.5(d) selects the greatest `observed_at`), the write commits normally, and zero of the five listings are rescored on the following tick.
11. **Negative control — L9 cannot be overwritten, asserted as a population invariant.** After cases 7–9, assert for every listing in `P` of that seller that no `listing_score` row exists whose group-3 `observed_at` names a deleted `seller_rating_observation`, and that a second quiet tick writes nothing anywhere. This is the whole-population form of the SA-007 failure and it fails loudly if a later change narrows `D_selected` back to the offer selector.

Cases 12–15 are the **phantom** half (SA-009). Each asserts the guarantee at the level the finding demands — **that no `listing_score` row derived from the deleted rating is ever written**, not merely that L9 later clears a pointer — by querying `listing_score` for any row whose group-3 seller `observed_at` equals the deleted row's, across the whole table and not only the current pointers:

12. **`P`-entry phantom, the reviewer's interleaving exactly.** Seed a listing for the seller with **no snapshot**, so it is outside `P`. Barrier: let `SellerRatingObservation.lock_parents_for_delete` select and lock the fan-out, then `append_snapshot` that listing into `P` on a second connection, then tick the refresh, then let the sweep's `DELETE` and L9 commit. Assert the refresh **blocks on the seller anchor** — not on the listing row, which the sweep never locked — that it writes nothing until the sweep commits, that the score it eventually writes cites a **surviving** rating or the S2-12 prior, and that **zero** `listing_score` rows anywhere cite the deleted `observed_at`. Assert explicitly that the phantom listing is **not** in the sweep's lock set, so the test proves the anchor and not the fan-out.
13. **Seller-reassignment phantom.** A listing already in `P` for seller `A` is reassigned to the swept seller `B` after `B`'s fan-out was selected. Same barrier and the same assertions, plus: the refresh that had already taken `A`'s anchor **abandons at (c)** on the re-read `seller_id` term rather than committing against the wrong anchor, and **C3** selects the listing on the next tick because `seller` is in `SCORING_INPUT_FIELDS`.
14. **Reassignment *away* from the swept seller.** A listing scored from rating `R` of seller `B` moves to seller `A` before the sweep selects `B`'s fan-out, so L9 never sees it. Assert its stale pointer is repaired by **C3** on the next tick and that its new score cites `A`'s aggregate — the miss is covered by a clause, not by the sweep.
15. **Negative control — an unrelated seller.** A listing entering `P` for a seller whose ratings are **not** being swept must not block on any anchor the sweep holds and must be scored on the same tick. Assert its refresh acquires a different `seller` row, completes while the sweep is mid-batch, and that the sweep's own fan-out is unchanged — so the anchor is proved to be seller-scoped rather than a global gate on scoring.

Canonicalization is pinned in `scoring/digest.py`: sorted keys, UTF-8, no insignificant whitespace, and **all floats quantized to 6 decimal places before serialization**. Without the quantization, ordinary floating-point noise would defeat idempotence.

**What actually makes a quiet system write nothing** (corrected in revision 3, review finding SA-014): not a staleness ceiling — that ceiling was the write amplifier, not the cure — but the two invariance properties above it. Between real dependency changes the decay weights differ only by a common positive factor, so `n_eff`, `λ`, `q` and the quantiles are numerically identical tick to tick; the §3.4.1 `baseline_digest` excludes recomputation identity so an unchanged cohort produces an unchanged digest; and `D` carries that digest rather than the baseline's row id, so no listing's `inputs_digest` moves either. Quantization then absorbs the last few ulps of floating-point noise. Nothing in this chain depends on how often the job ticks, which is why the 60 s interval of S2-3 is affordable.

**Tests (MS-2d):** change each explanation-only field in turn (a risk flag; the seller evidence counts; `dollars_per_tb` by a sub-rounding amount; the cohort key via relaxation; `matcher_grain`) while holding `deal_score`, the cap set, `is_provisional` and `algorithm_version` constant, and assert a new row **and** an updated `listing.current_*` pointer. Then repeat with a genuinely identical canonical payload and assert **zero** writes and an untouched `current_scored_at`.

#### 3.4.3 Immutable version dispatch (reproducing history)

Review finding SA-010; decision S2-13. Storing `algorithm_version` proves *which* algorithm ran; it does not keep that algorithm runnable. Once a constant changes and the version bumps, a golden test over the current version passes while every older row becomes underivable from the tree — and NFR-007 asks for reproducibility, not for a value in a column.

**Contract:**

Review round 3 found this contract still only **partially** resolved (SA-010). Revision 3 froze each version *directory* and protected its manifest entry against merge-base edits, which closed the shared-helper and editable-manifest holes. It left three doors open, all outside the frozen directory: historical execution still passed through the mutable `scoring/versions/__init__.py` registry, through `scoring/compose.py`'s wrapper behavior, and through `scoring/contracts.py`'s parsing, coercion and defaults — so changing dispatch or an input default could alter or disable a historical version while every directory digest still matched. Retirement was also proven only against "a management command's database", which an empty CI or development database satisfies vacuously. Items 1, 2 and 5 below close all three. **Round 4 then found two of them still open** (SA-010): revision 4's item 2 protected the registry but still executed through `compose.py`, and its item 5 checked only the *latest* backup while §18.6 retains six months of restorable dumps. Revision 5 closes both, in the same two items.

1. **Each released version is a package directory with a self-contained entry point, and nothing score-shaping is left outside it.** `scoring/versions/v<N>/` holds the complete constant set, every score-shaping helper it uses, **and its own parsing**. Its public surface is exactly one callable:

   ```python
   # Binding — the only cross-boundary contract a released version exposes.
   def score(D: dict) -> dict: ...
   ```

   `D` is the §3.4.1 canonical dependency document as **plain JSON-compatible data** — the same bytes `inputs_json` stores — and the return value is plain data too. **The version parses `D` itself**, with version-local validation, coercion and defaults; a field added to `D` after `v<N>` was released is ignored by `v<N>` because `v<N>`'s own parser does not know it, which is the behavior NFR-007 wants. Concretely, code under `v<N>/` may import only (a) the Python standard library and (b) other modules inside its own `v<N>/` package. **It may not import `scoring/contracts.py`, `stats.py`, `subscores.py`, `vetoes.py`, `cohort.py`, `digest.py`, `explain.py`, or any other shared module** — revision 3 permitted `contracts.py` on the grounds that it holds "data shapes with no score-shaping behavior", and that was the remaining hole: a Pydantic model carries validators, coercion rules and field defaults, so a default added there changes what a historical version computes for an input outside the golden fixtures. `scoring/contracts.py` is now the **current version's public façade only** — the typed shape MS-3 and the service layer consume — and the shared `stats.py` / `subscores.py` / `vetoes.py` / `cohort.py` / `explain.py` modules become thin current-version wrappers that delegate into the dispatched package. The duplication between `v1/` and `v2/` is the point: a released version's behavior stops depending on anything that can still be edited.

2. **The registry is static data, its digest is part of every version's manifest entry, and historical execution does not pass through `compose.py` at all.** `scoring/versions/__init__.py` is a plain mapping literal `ALGORITHM_VERSIONS = {"v1": v1, ...}` with no conditionals, no aliasing, no fallback and no default; an unknown `algorithm_version` raises rather than resolving to the newest.

   Round 4 found the remaining hole (SA-010): revision 4 still routed every call through `scoring/compose.py`, a mutable module outside every protected digest, which can preprocess `D` or postprocess the result while the manifest gate stays green. **The dispatcher therefore resolves the package from the registry and calls `ALGORITHM_VERSIONS[v].score(D)` directly.** Concretely:

   ```python
   # Binding — scoring/dispatch.py, the whole historical execution path.
   def score_with(version: str, D: dict) -> dict:
       return ALGORITHM_VERSIONS[version].score(D)   # no transform before, none after
   ```

   `scoring/dispatch.py` may contain **only** that lookup-and-call: no defaulting, no coercion, no merging, no post-processing, no logging of a transformed payload. `scoring/compose.py` becomes the **current-version façade only** — it builds `D` for a *new* score and returns the typed `contracts.py` shape MS-3 consumes — and it is never on the path of a historical re-derivation, which reads `inputs_json` and calls `score_with` directly. *Rejected alternative:* folding `compose.py` into every version's manifest digest. It would work, but it freezes a module whose whole purpose is to move with the current version, so every future release would have to fork it; and it leaves the wrapper *on* the historical path, which means the protection is a hash rather than an absence. Removing the module from the path is the stronger property: there is nothing to protect.

   The gate enforces the boundary the same way it enforces the import rule: `scripts.check` fails if `scoring/dispatch.py` contains any statement other than the registry lookup and the call, and fails if any code path that re-derives a stored score imports `compose.py`. `MANIFEST.json` records, per released version, **two** digests: the **directory digest** (SHA-256 over the canonical JSON of every file's repository-relative path and content hash under `v<N>/`, sorted by path — so adding, deleting or renaming a file changes it exactly as editing one does) **and the `registry_digest`**, the content hash of `versions/__init__.py`. Because the registry digest is recorded in *every* entry, **any** edit to the dispatch mapping invalidates **every** released entry at once, and the only way back to green is to write the new registry digest into each entry — which the merge-base rule below rejects for existing entries. Adding a `v(N+1)` therefore requires adding its entry *and* rewriting each existing entry's `registry_digest`, so the sanctioned path is explicit: the gate accepts an existing entry whose **only** difference from the merge base is a `registry_digest` update that matches the new registry, and rejects every other difference. That is the narrow, stated exception; without it the "additions only" rule would make adding a version impossible, and an author would end up loosening the rule instead.

   `scripts.check` enforces, against the manifest as it stands in the **merge base**:
   - an **existing** entry whose directory digest differs from the merge-base manifest is a **failure**, even when the package and its entry were edited together — this is the case revision 2 let through;
   - an existing entry whose recomputed directory digest differs from its recorded digest is a **failure**;
   - an existing entry whose `registry_digest` differs from the merge base **and** does not equal the current `versions/__init__.py` hash is a **failure**; equal is accepted only when the registry change is purely additive (every merge-base key still maps to the same package) — a key that is **removed or repointed** fails regardless;
   - the **import-boundary check**: any import inside `v<N>/` outside the standard library and its own package is a **failure**;
   - **additions are the only other sanctioned change**, and a **removal** fails unless the version appears in an explicit `retired` allowlist in the same manifest, which item 5's proof is what justifies adding to.

3. **Every version's golden fixtures run on every change** — `tests/golden/scoring/v<N>/` for each released `N`, not just the current one. With items 1 and 2 in force these fixtures are a **regression net, not the immutability mechanism**: a shared-helper edit, a parsing default, and a registry repoint can no longer reach a released version at all, so the fixtures no longer have to be complete enough to catch them. That inversion is the substance of the SA-010 fix — revision 2 asked finite fixtures to prove an infinite property.
4. The constant set a version owns: the four component weights, the `0.02` subscore floor, the three cap values, the veto trigger predicates, the half-life and window, the `λ` target of 30, the seller prior `μ₀`/`κ` and Wilson `z`, the fitness sub-weights and rubric tables, the availability rubric's interior values, `CAPACITY_BUCKETS_TB` and its 5% tolerance, the parent-tier map, `QUANTITY_MIN_CONFIDENCE`, `MAX_LOT_QUANTITY`, the rounding mode, and the digest canonicalization rules.
5. **Retirement is an operational procedure, and CI cannot perform it.** Revision 3 said "a management command shows no `listing_score` row cites it" without saying *which database*, and an empty CI or development database answers "none" for every version — so the check could pass while production and every restorable backup still held citing `merchant_fact` rows. Retirement is therefore defined as a **runbook step against every authoritative dataset**, not a gate:

   - The **authoritative datasets** are (i) the production database (`docs/handoff/deployed.md`) and (ii) **every retained restorable backup** — master-spec §18.6 retains **48 hourly, 14 daily, 8 weekly and 6 monthly** dumps, and a restore of *any* of them reintroduces the rows it holds. Revision 4 said "the latest restorable backup", which review finding SA-010 correctly reads as leaving up to six months of restorable citations unchecked: retire a version today and restore last month's dump tomorrow, and the restored scores are underivable.
   - **The retirement rule, stated as a date comparison rather than a scan.** Restoring every retained dump to prove a negative is not operationally reasonable, and it is not necessary. Let `t_last` be the instant the **last citing row** left the production database (its deletion or expiry, recorded when the operator observes the zero count), and let `t_oldest` be the `pg_dump` timestamp of the **oldest retained dump** — under the §18.6 schedule, up to six months old. Then:

     ```text
     retirable(v)  ⇔  production citations(v) = 0
                      AND t_oldest > t_last          -- every retained dump was taken after the last citation was gone
     ```

     A version is therefore not retirable until the retention schedule has rolled the last citing dump out of the set — in the worst case six months after the last citing row was deleted. The rule is a comparison of two recorded timestamps, so it is auditable from the PR without restoring anything; a restore is required only when the operator cannot establish `t_last` from the production record and must fall back to citation counts against individually restored dumps.
   - `manage.py scoring_version_citations --database-url <URL> --version v<N>` runs the citation count **against a named database URL** and prints the count, the dataset it was run against, and the timestamp. It takes no default and refuses to run against the configured application database implicitly, so "which database" is always recorded rather than assumed. The URL is supplied from the environment or the operator's secret store and is **never** written into this repository, a plan, or a runbook — only the variable name is (public-repo rule).
   - Retirement is **refused** while production reports a nonzero count, and refused while `t_oldest ≤ t_last`. The operator records in the PR that adds the `retired` allowlist entry: the production count and its timestamp, `t_last` with the evidence for it, `t_oldest` and the backup-set identifiers it came from, and any individually restored dump they had to fall back to. The gate then checks only that the allowlist entry exists; the manifest entry preserves the recorded counts, timestamps and dataset identifiers.
   - **`scripts.check` cannot prove retirement and does not try.** It has no access to production or to a restored backup, and giving CI that access to satisfy a hygiene check would be a far worse trade than keeping a version directory. The gate's job is to make deletion *impossible without the allowlist entry*; the allowlist entry's job is to record that a human ran the proof.
   - Because bounded eBay `listing_score` rows expire under S2-2a while `merchant_fact` rows do not, a version cited by any non-eBay score is effectively permanent. That is the intended outcome, and §3.3.5(f) makes the same statement about baseline versions.

*Rejected alternative:* a per-row reproducibility manifest embedding the constant set in `inputs_json`. Rejected because the constants are only half the algorithm: the rubric structure, the relaxation order, the rounding mode and the digest canonicalization are code, not values, and a row cannot carry them. A manifest would make the constants auditable while still leaving the score underivable — the appearance of NFR-007 compliance without the substance.

**Tests (MS-2d) — each of the three SA-010 escape routes gets its own negative control:**

1. Persist fixtures under `v1`; introduce a `v2` that changes a constant; assert both `v1` and `v2` rows re-derive **exactly** through dispatch from `inputs_json` alone.
2. Edit a file inside `scoring/versions/v1/` **and** update its manifest entry to match; assert the gate still goes **red** against the merge-base manifest. (Revision 2's check passed this.)
3. Change a shared helper's behavior for an input **absent from every golden fixture**; assert the gate goes red — under item 1 it fails at the import boundary, because no released version may import that helper at all. Add an import from `scoring/stats.py` inside `v1/`, and separately from `scoring/contracts.py`, and assert the import check fails for both.
4. **Registry negative controls (SA-010's `suggested_validation`, first half).** Repoint `ALGORITHM_VERSIONS["v1"]` at the `v2` package and assert the gate goes red on the `registry_digest` rule with **no** file inside `v1/` changed. Remove the `v1` key entirely and assert the same. Then add a `v3` key and assert the gate goes green **only** when every existing entry's `registry_digest` was updated to the new registry hash and every directory digest is untouched.
5. **Parsing negative control.** Add a new field with a default to `scoring/contracts.py`, and separately change an existing field's coercion, for an input outside every golden fixture; assert `v1`'s output through dispatch is **byte-identical**, because `v1` parses `D` itself and never imports that module.
6. Delete `scoring/versions/v1/` without an allowlist entry and assert the gate goes red.
7. **Retirement negative control (SA-010's `suggested_validation`, second half).** Seed a scratch dataset with one `merchant_fact` `listing_score` row citing `v1`; run `scoring_version_citations --database-url` against it and assert a nonzero count and a nonzero exit status, so the runbook step fails closed. **Then the older-backup case the round-4 finding names:** with production reporting **zero** citations, seed the citation into a restored dataset standing in for a retained monthly dump and assert the runbook step still refuses — and, as its date-rule equivalent, assert the check refuses whenever `t_oldest ≤ t_last`, so the refusal does not depend on anyone thinking to restore that particular dump. Assert the command **refuses to run without an explicit `--database-url`**. Assert that `scripts.check` alone, with the allowlist entry present, never inspects any database — the gate's green is conditional on a recorded human proof, and the test asserts the gate makes no database connection at all.
8. **Dispatch negative controls (SA-010 round 4).** Mutate `scoring/compose.py` to transform `D` before scoring, and separately to transform the returned payload; assert a stored `v1` score still re-derives **byte-identically**, because the re-derivation path calls `ALGORITHM_VERSIONS["v1"].score(D)` through `dispatch.py` and never enters `compose.py`. Then add any statement other than the lookup-and-call to `dispatch.py` and assert `scripts.check` goes **red**, and assert the same for a re-derivation path that imports `compose.py`.

#### 3.4.4 The global-run schema (`ScoringRun`)

Review finding SA-013, blocking. Revision 2 said the refresh is explicitly **cross-source** and then proposed to record it as `RunKind.SCORE` on `ScraperRun`. That is not implementable: `ScraperRun.source_site` is a non-null `PROTECT` foreign key to `SourceSite` (`catalog/models/ops.py:206-225`), so every row must name one merchant. The three ways out of that were a nullable `source_site`, a sentinel non-merchant `SourceSite` row, and a separate model. **Decision S2-19 takes the separate model.**

*Alternatives rejected.* **Nullable `source_site`** — it weakens an invariant that currently holds for every acquisition run, and it silently changes the meaning of the existing `("source_site", "-started_at")` index and of every source-health query that assumes a run has a source; the cost lands on MS-1 code that has nothing to do with scoring. **A sentinel `SourceSite`** — a fake merchant in a table whose rows carry retention class, licensing posture, and poll cadence is exactly the "invent a fake source" failure the reviewer named, and it would appear in operator source lists and cadence reports as a source that cannot be polled. A separate model costs one migration and buys both invariants intact.

**Shape.** `ScoringRun` in `catalog/models/ops.py`, a plain `models.Model` alongside `ScraperRun` (operational telemetry only — it holds counts, not merchant content, so it is deliberately **not** `RetentionGoverned`, matching `ScraperRun`'s own posture):

| Field | Type | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `started_at` | `DateTimeField` | Set when the refresh begins |
| `finished_at` | `DateTimeField(null=True, blank=True)` | NULL while running or after a hard crash |
| `status` | `CharField(choices=RunStatus.choices, default=RUNNING)` | **Reuses** the existing `RunStatus` |
| `failure_class` | `CharField(choices=RunFailureClass.choices, blank=True, default="")` | **Reuses** the existing `RunFailureClass` |
| `algorithm_version` | `CharField` | Which version this run executed |
| `cohorts_evaluated` | `PositiveIntegerField(default=0)` | Cohorts whose dirtiness was checked |
| `cohorts_refreshed` | `PositiveIntegerField(default=0)` | Cohorts that were actually recomputed (B1–B6 fired) |
| `baseline_writes` | `PositiveIntegerField(default=0)` | Baselines whose `baseline_digest` actually changed |
| `listings_rescored` | `PositiveIntegerField(default=0)` | Listings whose score was recomputed |
| `score_writes` | `PositiveIntegerField(default=0)` | New `listing_score` rows appended (§3.4.2) |
| `baseline_versions_pruned` | `PositiveIntegerField(default=0)` | Superseded, uncited baseline versions reclaimed by the §3.3.5(g) sweep; zero on ticks where the sweep did not run |
| `error` | `TextField(blank=True, default="")` | Same shape as `ScraperRun.error` |
| `detail_json` | `JSONField(default=dict, blank=True)` | Per-cohort failure detail for the isolation path |

`Meta.db_table = "scoring_run"`, one index on `("-started_at",)`. There is **no** `source_site` field and no run-kind discriminator — the model *is* the discriminator.

The counts are what make the SA-014 no-drift claim auditable in production rather than only in a test: on a quiet tick `cohorts_evaluated` is positive while `cohorts_refreshed`, `baseline_writes`, `listings_rescored` and `score_writes` are all zero. `baseline_versions_pruned` is reported separately because pruning is storage hygiene, not scoring work, and a nonzero value on an otherwise quiet tick is expected rather than a drift signal. Together with §3.3.5(h), the pair `baseline_writes` / `baseline_versions_pruned` is the production measurement of baseline-version growth.

**Source-health queries are unaffected by construction.** Every cadence, lane, freshness and failure-rate query in MS-1 reads `ScraperRun` (or `SourceLaneState`), and `ScoringRun` is a different table with no relation to `SourceSite`, so no existing query can see a scoring run and none needs a `run_kind != score` filter. That is the concrete advantage over the nullable and sentinel options, both of which would have required auditing every such query.

**Tests (MS-2d, live DB):**

1. Persist a **successful** `ScoringRun` with no merchant source at all; assert the row saves, `status = success`, `finished_at` set, and the six counts equal the refresh's actual work.
2. Persist a **failed** `ScoringRun` with a `RunFailureClass`; assert it saves with no source and that a per-cohort failure leaves `status = success` with the failure recorded in `detail_json` (the §4 isolation posture), while a whole-run failure sets `status = failed`.
3. **Negative control on the untouched invariant:** constructing a `ScraperRun` without `source_site` still raises; existing acquisition runs continue to require a source.
4. **Non-interference:** run one scoring refresh, then assert every source-health and cadence query returns exactly what it returned before the refresh — the scoring run is invisible to them.

### MS-2e — Real inputs, the glass box, and coverage

- **eBay adapter extension** — one change covering three fields: `seller.feedbackPercentage` and `seller.feedbackScore` into `SellerRatingObservation` **under the §3.2.5 normalization contract, with the dedupe rule of §3.2.5(d) so an unchanged republication writes no row**, and return terms into `Listing.returns_policy` / `returns_window_days`. Today `acquisition/sources/ebay.py` extracts the seller **username only** (`_seller_username`) and parses no return terms whatsoever. Cassette-backed, PII-scrubbed per DR-006.
  - **Plan-time investigation, flagged rather than assumed:** the adapter consumes the Browse `item_summary/search` response. Whether return terms are available on that response, or require a per-item Browse `getItem` call, must be confirmed against current eBay documentation and a live cassette **before** the MS-2e plan commits to it — a per-item call would add one request per listing per poll and would need its own quota and cadence analysis. Until that is confirmed, eBay `returns_policy` stays `unknown`, which under S2-8 means a flag and no cap. The round-1 document asserted "eBay Browse exposes `returnTerms`" as settled; it is not, and MS-2 must not plan on it.
  - **Second plan-time confirmation, same gate (§3.2.5a):** whether `seller.feedbackScore` on that response is a **total** feedback count or a **net** score (positives minus negatives). The repository's only evidence is the buyer-facing description at `docs/research/principled-deal-score-for-hard-drive-listings.md:101`, which describes the displayed bracketed number, not the API field; no cassette exercises it, because `acquisition/sources/ebay.py:114-120` extracts the username only. §3.2.5 states the contract for both readings and pins `count_basis` so the choice is recorded per observation rather than assumed globally. The MS-2e plan records the disposition; nothing else in this document depends on it.
- Per-source `returns_policy` population for the four merchant sources (site-level policy constants) and `seller_policy_prior` seeding (S2-12).
- **DR-004 completeness test:** every `listing_score` row carries input facts used, `algorithm_version`, thresholds and margins, confidence (`n_eff` / `λ` + the provisional marker), risk flags, the machine-readable payload, and the user-facing text. FR-006's percentile **+ margin-in-IQR**, seller evidence, fitness rubric pieces, quantity provenance, the relaxation path, and any cap reason are all present — MS-3 renders this payload; it does not compute anything.
- **Coverage report:** per-source model-grain-or-better percentage against C.3.5's **≥ 80% expectation**. Because §3.3.3 makes the SMR veto model-grain-only, this report is also the measurement of how much of the SMR protection is actually in force — the report states both figures. A shortfall is reported and routed to the catalog/matcher backfill queue (`unknown_model_backfill`), never treated as an MS-2 blocker and never a reason to loosen ADR 0019's precision contract.
- **Baseline-composition report (S2-2b):** the share of in-scope listings whose cohort baseline is thin or exhausted, so the cost of the conservative eBay boundary is a measured number the owner can weigh against the ratified S2-2b exclusion — whose named alternative, a bounded eBay-inclusive aggregate, stays available as a later decision.

**MS-2 exit = spec §19 MS-2 acceptance (line 916), quantified over `P` (§2.2):** every listing in `P` has a 0–100 score reproducible from stored inputs with a per-factor breakdown; thin-cohort listings (`n_eff < 30`) visibly shrink toward neutral and are marked provisional; the documented cohort-relaxation fallback fires on small cohorts and records which step fired; the ≥ 80% model-grain coverage figure is measured and reported as an expectation.

## 4. Error handling & ops (MS-2 posture)

- The `score-refresh` job writes one **`ScoringRun`** row per run (§3.4.4, S2-19) — status, cohorts evaluated and refreshed, baseline writes, listings rescored, score writes, runtime, failure class (NFR-004). Scoring becomes visible in the same operational place acquisition already is, **without** borrowing `ScraperRun`'s per-source shape.
- A per-cohort failure is isolated (MS-2d); a total refresh failure surfaces as a failed `ScoringRun` and, from MS-4, an operator alert. **No email alerts until MS-4** (ADR 0013) — the MS-1 posture is unchanged: DB rows, structured logs, and the §18.5 dead-man's switch.
- `max_instances=1` + `coalesce=True` are already the poller's `job_defaults`; scoring inherits them, so a slow refresh degrades to lower frequency rather than to concurrent double-writes. **That is a per-job-id guarantee only, and revision 9 stops treating it as more than one** (review finding SA-006 of audit `b2eedc33`): `acquisition/scheduling/apply.py:14-19` states in as many words that separate job ids can be inside the same code at the same time, and `poller/service.py:337-342` registers `retention-sweep` as its own job, so `score-refresh` and the sweep **do** overlap. §14's "single user; no locking/queueing design needed" therefore holds **within** the refresh and not **across** the two jobs. §3.4.2's serialization contract is the cross-job rule: the `listing` row is the single lock, taken first on every path, with no queue and no new state (S2-4 is preserved — a lock is not a queue). **Revision 10 widens what it protects** (review finding SA-007 of audit `b2eedc33`): the commit-time revalidation covers **every** selected child input of `D` — the offer snapshot and the seller rating — not the offer selector alone, and the deleting side must lock every listing whose decision the deleted rows feed, which for a seller rating is a fan-out over that seller's listings in `P` rather than one FK parent. The fan-out adds no writes: it is the set L9's clear already updates in the same transaction. **Revision 11 adds one lock class above it** (review finding SA-009): the `seller` row is the anchor a rating's deleter and a rating's consumer both take, because the fan-out is a mutable predicate and locking its current members cannot exclude a listing that joins later. The order is `seller` → `listing`, ascending within each class, and the refresh's share lock on a near-static `Seller` row is the whole added contention — measured by MS-2d, with an advisory lock on the seller id as the named alternative if it is material. Its cost is bounded and named: the sweep may wait on one refresh's per-listing transaction, and MS-2d measures that wait against the retention sweep's own budget, with the abandon-only fallback recorded in §3.4.2 and its residual in §6.
- **Capacity watch:** `listing_score` **and the §3.3.5 append-only `cohort_baseline` versions** are new write paths onto the same disk as `offer_snapshot` and the raw payloads. The §3.4.2 decision digest is the whole bound, and revision 3 makes it a real one: with B7 withdrawn (SA-014) a quiet 60 s tick performs bounded detection queries and writes nothing at all, instead of appending a row per affected listing every hour. Growth is now proportional to genuine input change, which is the property the storage rationale always claimed. The MS-1d disk-space alert (CT-116) covers the growth, and the TimescaleDB-aware dump pipeline must be confirmed to include the new tables before the first production refresh — the same pre-real-data check MS-1d ran, re-verified rather than assumed. **Baseline versions are the second bound and the less certain one:** §3.3.5(h) derives an order-10¹ MB/day estimate at v1 scale and makes the MS-2c measurement a precondition for enabling MS-2d in production, with the §3.3.5(g) prune sweep and the `baseline_versions_pruned` count as the ongoing evidence. **Revision 5 changes what that number rests on rather than the number itself** (review finding SA-021): revision 4 derived it from price events while its own definition of `E` produced a version per **poll**, i.e. order 10³ MB/day on an unchanging system — so §4 was carrying an estimate the mechanism could not deliver. The S2-25 event definition makes the version rate proportional to actual price movement, which is what the estimate always assumed. It remains a derivation from this document's own assumed cohort sizes, not a measurement, and it is still the weakest number in §4 until MS-2c reports. **Revision 7 widens the version identity without moving the estimate** (review finding SA-025): the semantic `source_composition` joins `baseline_digest`, adding a minting term that fires on a source rename or re-attribution — administrative events orders below the price-change rate — while the two poll-cadence quantities revision 6 kept on the version, `raw_snapshot_count` and `next_window_exit_at`, move to the pointer in the same revision (§3.3.5b, §3.3.5h).
- **Refresh cost has a scan in it, and it is named:** B3's grouped `price_event_digest` query is the only unindexed step of the dirtiness pass (§3.4.1C). MS-2d records its share of the 60 s budget; the tunable if it dominates is `B3_INTERVAL`, which lags detection without losing it. B1's ingestion high-water is what keeps the *refresh* off that path in the common case, and its §2.2.2 commit-lag margin means a just-arrived snapshot can be one tick late — an acceptable trade against a permanently skipped row.
- **Version retirement is an operator runbook step, not a job and not a gate** (§3.4.3 item 5): the citation proof runs against the production database and against **the whole retained backup set** (master-spec §18.6 — 48 hourly, 14 daily, 8 weekly, 6 monthly), reduced to the `t_oldest > t_last` date comparison so no operator has to restore six months of dumps to prove a negative. `scripts.check` deliberately has access to none of them. The practical consequence, stated because it is a real cost: **a version stays on disk for up to six months after its last citing row is deleted**, because that is how long a dump that still cites it remains restorable. A version whose directory still exists costs kilobytes; a version deleted while a restorable backup still cites it costs the reproducibility NFR-007 asks for.
- An `algorithm_version` bump triggers a full rescore; at v1 scale that is a single long refresh, not a migration, and it is announced in the sub-milestone PR that bumps it.
- The §2.3(b) clearing hooks run inside existing transactions; a crash between the delist mark and the clear is impossible by construction, and the MS-2a test asserts the two cannot be observed separately.

## 5. Testing strategy

- **Unit (no DB) — the bulk of MS-2:** the entire `scoring/` pure library. Table-driven fixtures reproduce the ADR-0011 mock-data archetypes and their published bands (re-derived under the §3.2.1 half-up rounding); each of the three caps has a fires/does-not-fire pair **and a binding assertion at exactly 35 / 60**; `n_eff` and `λ` carry the §3.2.3 property set plus the non-monotonicity counterexample fixture; the §3.2.2 quantity table is exhaustive; **the §3.2.4 rubric fixtures are exhaustive by construction — 30 HDD `T`, 25 SSD `T`, 24 `W`, 28 `C` and 4 availability cases, with the case counts asserted against the enum members themselves so a later enum addition fails until its cell exists (SA-011)**; **the §3.3.4 rank fixtures cover cheapest, most expensive, duplicated price, all-equal (`q = 0.5` exactly), between-quantile, and the non-member eBay case (SA-012)**; **the §3.3.4 quartile fixtures are exhaustive over the shapes that broke revision 6's inverse — singleton, all-equal, two-point unequal weight, heavy terminal tie and heavy leading tie — each asserting all three quartiles, the IQR state and `margin_iqr`, plus the sign-agreement property against `q` (SA-026)**; the §3.3.2 relaxation table covers both adjacency directions, bucket ends, unknown values, **the four ad-hoc/tie fixtures with their exact keys and full terminal output (SA-004)**, each first-succeeding step, and both exhaustion outcomes; the seller prior has an explicit **0.50 boundary** test (S2-12); **the §3.2.5 seller-normalization table is exhaustive over its input cases and its aggregation rules — latest-observation selection out of insertion order, identical-republication dedupe, decreasing counts used-with-a-flag, and the unusable-observation path to the S2-12 prior (SA-016)**; **the §3.2.6 provisional matrix asserts all four `n_eff` × warranty combinations plus one case per `provisional_reasons` value (SA-019), and the neutral-payload case asserting stored `n_eff = 0` / `λ = 0` against NULL `q`, quartiles and `margin_iqr` (SA-027)**; the geometric mean has a floor test proving `max(s_k, 0.02)` prevents a zero subscore from annihilating the product; `scoring/digest.py` has canonicalization tests (key order, float quantization, byte-identical round trip).
- **DB (live TimescaleDB):** the `listing_score` hypertable DDL and retention constraints (including the eBay-classed row that **must** carry `expires_at`); the §2.3 source-composition tests and **the six conditional-clearing tests, led by the SA-002 regression (an expired old score row plus a newer valid current row: the old row is deleted and the current pointer survives)**; the 90-day windowed non-eBay pull; relaxation over seeded thin cohorts; **the §3.3.4 reproduction test — discard the in-memory observations, recompute every `q` from `observation_vector` alone, assert exact equality, then advance the clock an hour and assert it is still exact (SA-012 and SA-014 in one fixture)**; each §3.4.1 trigger in isolation with clock advancement, **including the three-step no-drift test (one hour without a boundary crossing ⇒ identical statistics, zero baseline write, zero score write; then cross `next_window_exit_at` ⇒ B2 refresh) (SA-014)**; **the L2 provenance test (change only `confidence` / `layer` / `source_text` and assert a new decision) and the B5 same-grain retarget test (SA-003)**; **the §3.3.5 append-only baseline tests — the SA-015 historical re-derivation after a cohort change with the snapshots and the newer vector out of reach, `UPDATE` refusal on a released version, digest-identical refresh inserting no row, and the prune rule including the `ProtectedError` path (SA-015)**; **the §3.3.4 grain tests — many identical-price snapshots leave the baseline clean immediately after computation, deleting a non-boundary snapshot of a run keeps it clean, and deleting a run-start snapshot or repricing refreshes exactly the affected baselines (SA-017)**; **the §2.2.1 selector tests — out-of-order arrivals, a newest snapshot with NULL `fx_rate`, deletion of the selected row firing L8, and every-eligible-snapshot-deleted reaching `price_unavailable` (SA-018)**; **the §3.4.4 `ScoringRun` tests, including the negative control that a `ScraperRun` still cannot be saved without a source (SA-013)**; **the §2.2.2 ingestion tests — a backfilled snapshot older than `last_evaluated_at` fires B1, an insert before a run start creates exactly one new version, an insert inside a run creates none, and `append_snapshot` cannot set `ingested_at` (SA-020)**; **the §3.3.4 boundary test — 90 days plus one hour of minute-spaced identical prices crossed minute by minute yields zero versions and zero rescores (SA-021)**; **the §3.4.1 L9 fan-out tests, including the non-selected-row negative control (SA-022)**; **the §3.4.1 B5 cohort-move tests — four single-attribute changes at a fixed resolution id, each refreshing both cohorts, plus the emptied-cohort case (SA-023)**; **the §3.3.5(c) constraint tests for every neutral `price_basis` value (SA-024)**; **the §3.3.5 stamp-placement tests — a digest-identical evaluation advancing the pointer's `next_window_exit_at` and clearing B2, a provenance-only B5 change clean on the next tick, and a source re-attribution minting exactly one new version with its own `source_composition` (SA-025)**;
